=================
(L) Obscure Code.
=================
; Code to determine if CPU = Zilog Z80 or NEC D780C.
; Taken from ZX spectrum.

	; F3 and F5 are undocumented bits 3 and 5 
	; of the flag register

	xor	a
	dec	a		; F3 = 1, f5 = 1
	ld	a, 0		; A=0
	scf			; on Z80: F3 |= A, F5 |= A, on NEC: F3=A & ?, F5=A & ?
	push	af
	dec	sp
	pop	af
	inc	sp
	and	%00101000

	; z  = NEC D780C
	; NZ = Z80



; Get VZ Char POS. (0-511)                     
BASIC : 	X=28672 - (peek(30752)+(peek(30753)*256))
ASM	:	ld	hl, 30753
		ld	a, (hl)
		ld	c, a
		ld	hl, 30752
		ld	a, (hl)
		ld	b, a
		ld	hl, 28672
		sub	bc		; <--- FIX THIS
		
		Char pos (0-511) = HL



[Halt] [IM0] [IM1] [IM2]
The halt instruction tells the z80 to backstep the PC and perform a new fetch cycle 
(repeating the halt instruction) until an INT or NMI is received. This is done to 
permit the z80's dram refresh to continue working while the CPU is halted. Halt isn't 
interrupt mode specific. On the VZ NMI is tied high leaving only INT. The INT mode set
 up by the ROM on the VZ is IM1, a hardware triggered RST38. IM0 (interrupting device 
drops a single byte instruction onto the data bus, commonly a RST) and IM2 
(interrupting device drops a single byte onto the bus which the z80 uses as a vector 
into a interrupt handler table) are way beyond the simplicity of the VZ. Poking the 
instructions to ORG $7B00 would likely cause a crash when executed as that area of 
memory is the start of the basic program area and basic will overwrite parts of it 
before the user can jump to it using a basic command (usr(x) etc). There would have 
to be a ret / di+halt / loop of some sort added to the two instructions I provided 
otherwise the z80 will end up off in the weeds and the VZ will crash too.




#####################################################################################################

==========
(M) SOUND
==========

Sound
-------
Sound and music on the VZ was initially very limited to the beeps and bops from BASIC's SOUND command, 
as well as the few compiled and assembled examples of peoples experiments in the early days.

Sound is generated from the physical 74LS174 latch chip on the VZ200, whilst in the VZ300 it is part
of U14 being the GA004 chip.  We make sound by toggling bits 0 and 5 and at specific frequency, which
drives the internal peizo speaker (and some further trickery within emulators)
Since the VZ has a two bit sounding operation, we actually have : 
 - silence (-/-)
 - half volume (+/+)		That is, bit 5 goes from - to + and bit 0 from - to + (or viceversa)
 - and full volume (+/-)	Full volume requires both bit 0 and 5 to be each inversed. B0: 0-->1 and B5:1-->0

And of course, by toggling bits $6800/bit 1 and 2, we can actually send our sound routine out through the cassette port!

The BEEP routine is located at ROM $3450 and sound with pitch and duration located at $345C.
The reference manual shows us to make the keyboard beep to perform the call routine in BASIC
Thusly in BASIC, Rascal, Z88 or ASM, one would perform :
POKE30862,80:POKE30863,52:X=USR(X)

...and for simple "beyond BASIC" sound to use assembly:
	LD	HL, 230		; Pitch
	LD	BC, 50		; Duration
	CALL	$345C		; Call ROM routine.


76489 emulation
---------------
The SN76489AN is a 1980's dedicated sound chip found in a few 8 bit computers of the era.
An external DIY "Compumuse" kit was available to be built for most 8 bit computers - and the kit
used the 76489 chip. A few were built for the VZ which produced an enhanced tone and simple voice
channels for the VZ. It was designed to be plugged into any parallel printer port and with the 
correct OUT statements, sound and music was possible. 
The VZEM emulator can emulate output from the 76489 chip.


AY Sound
--------
The AY chip is a 3 channel music chip from the early 1980's used in 8 bit computers of the era.
A few external DIY kit designs are available for the user to be able to create enhanced music
from their 8 bit computer.

VZEM release 2009 is the only current emulator that will emulate AY sound for the VZ.
James Diffendaffer wrote a small tune 'Forest' for the specific 2009 release of VZEM AY emulation.
AY emulated ports for VZEM are:
Port 129 : Register number selection.
Port 128 : Data for Register.

OUT (129), _regsiter
OUT (128), _data

The standard AY registers are as follows:

    0x00: Channel 1 fine pitch
    0x01: Channel 1 coarse pitch (4 bits)
    0x02: Channel 2 fine pitch
    0x03: Channel 2 coarse pitch (4 bits)
    0x04: Channel 3 fine pitch
    0x05: Channel 3 coarse pitch (4 bits)
    0x06: Noise pitch (5 bits)
    0x07: Mixer
    0x08: Channel 1 volume (4 bits) / Envelope enable (1 bit)
    0x09: Channel 2 volume (4 bits) / Envelope enable (1 bit)
    0x0A: Channel 3 volume (4 bits) / Envelope enable (1 bit)
    0x0B: Envelope fine duration
    0x0C: Envelope coarse duration
    0x0D: Envelope shape (4 bits)


"1-bit" music
-------------
Coming straight from the ZX Spectrum, a few 1-bit sound driver music players have been ported over
to work on the VZ. Some of the players are quite sophisticatedly written and require every last cycle
of the VZ's CPU to bring some incredbile sounding music out of the humble VZ. I would like to think
that the original designers of the VZ never knew that music such as this was ever possible.

Interestingly a few of the music players output the bits in such a fashion that they do not drive the
internal pizeo speaker physically enough to produce a sound (too faint). Or they are only driving bit 0 [OR] bit 5.
To play at high-volume, requires bits 0 and 5 to be XOR'd (inversed) on each single iteration.
This causes the piezo to fully elongate back and forth.

By bypassing or putting into parallel with the piezo speaker using two 0.1uf caps on each polarity, 
plugging in an amplified speaker, the sound is quite delightful. The caps will protect from any
dangerous five volts passing either from the VZ to the amp or from the amp back to the VZ.
The editor has destroyed a VZ years ago by not doing this.


a. Speaker
----------
The VZ has two bit sound; that is, utilises bit 0 and bit 5 on latch $6800 to push-pull the piezo speaker.
With the two bits, there is the availability to have half volume and full volume sound/music.
The "latch" in the VZ200 is a physical chip that talks directly to the likes of the cassette port, 6847 video chip,
keyboar and to the speaker. In the VZ300 this is built into one of the GA custom chips. 
The memory address of this 'latch' is at $6800.

The working of the speaker can be deduced directly from the circuit diagram: the two bits push the speaker 
in opposite directions, so when it's "11" one bit is pushing the other is pulling and the result is zero. 
You can think of them as being +1/ 0, and -1/0. The sum is the position of the speaker. So in total there 
are two bits but only three possible positions:
0= bits "00" or "11"
-1 = bits "01"
+1 = bits "10"

Antonino Porcino:
I checked on the real machine the speaker bits we discussed days ago. There are indeed three 
possible levels: MAX, HALF and SILENCE. 
I wrote a program that checks all possible ways of toggling the two bits, it's here:
The MAX volume is when you toggle from 0,1 to 1,0. All others are half volume. The silence is when you have 1,1 or 0,0.




A copy of the current contents of $6800 is stored at memory location : $783B, which can be utilised in various functions.



	LD A,($783B)	; correct method for use for VZ internal speaker.
	XOR 21H		; read 30779 (copy of latch), wiggle bits in A from 1 to 33 and vice versa.
	LD ($6800),A	; write it out to latch.



   AND	33		; Mask sound input data to be only bit 0 and bit 5. Allows on or off on both or either bits.
   XOR	33		; Force an invert of the data which will force an invert of the piezo speaker when sent.
			; If bit5=0 and bit0=0, then an XOR 33 will make bit5=1 and bit0=1.  00->33 (half volume)
			; If bit5=1 and bit0=0, then an XOR 33 will make bit5=0 and bit0=1.  32->01 (Full volume)
			; If bit5=0 and bit0=1, then an XOR 33 will make bit5=1 and bit0=0.  01->32 (Full volume)
			; If bit5=1 and bit0=1, then an XOR 33 will make bit5=0 and bit0=0.  33->00 (half volume)
   LD	($6800), A	; Send the music data out to Speaker.

Suffice to say :

 	AND 33 to filter bits 0 & 5.
 	If A=00, then XOR 33.   --> 33 (half volume)
 	If A=01, then XOR 33.   --> 32 (full volume)
 	if A=32, then XOR 33.   --> 01 (full volume)
 	if A=33, then XOR 33.   --> 00 (half volume)


 AND 33/XOR 33  :  full volume only when data is 01 or 32. 		bit 1 or bit 5.
		   half volume on the music data when it is 00 or 33	0 or bits 1 & 5.

However, in real life examples, for whatever physical reasons, an XOR 33 will only work for external speakers 
and will be totally silent for the internal peizo speaker.

XOR 32 works for the internal speaker.


; ------------------------------------
;
;	and 	33		mask only bits 0 & 5	\
;	xor	32		toggle bit 5 only	|   FOR USE FOR INTERNAL speaker ONLY. 
;	ld	($6800), a	output to latch		/
;
; ------------------------------------
;
;							
;	.						\   External speaker.
;	xor 33			toggle bits 0 & 5	|
;	ld 	($6800), a	output to latch		/
;
; ------------------------------------


B. Masking for MODE(1)
----------------------
Playing 1-bit music with MODE(1) enabled requires bit 3 to be enabled.
After masking register A music data for bits 0 and 5, bit 3 needs to be enabled before sending the music data
to the latch.

	bit 3:		0 = MODE(0)
			1 = MODE(1)

	bit 4:		0 = "COLOR,0". Green background.	
			1 = "COLOR,1". Orange background in MODE(0), buff background in MODE(1).

	exmaple : continuously in mode(1) with 1-bit music playing. "COLOR,0" background.
	AND	33			; masking music data for bits 0&5.
	OR	8			; enable mode(1) bit. Green background.
	LD	($6800), A		; write reg-A data to latch.


	exmaple : continuously in mode(1) with 1-bit music playing. "COLOR,1" background.
	AND	33			; masking music data for bits 0&5.
	OR	24			; enable mode(1) bit 3 -> decimal 8. 
					; enable buff backgrnd for mode(1) bit 4 -> decimal 16.   
					; 8d logical "OR" 16d = 24d.
	LD	($6800), A		; write reg-A data to latch.





c. Cassette for sound output
----------------------------
The VZ can also output music through to the cassette port.
Simply plug in an amplified speaker into the 3.5mm stereo port, and *walla*, music can be played.
The cassette port utilises bits $1 and $2 for a digital 'analogue-equivalent' 'push-pull' signal through the cassette port.
If A has normal output-to-speaker music data, then by ANDing A with $6 and then passing reg A to port $6800, this will 
write the music data through to the cassette port.
Data sent to the latch for cassette use can be zero or a one. This is by setting bit 2 which is the value of decimal 4.
OR
Setting bit 1 and bit 2 (value of 6) also works.

BIT #	desc		weighting value		
 0	Speaker		1 or 0
 1	LSB TAPE	2 or 0
 2	MSB TAPE	4 or 0

MASK value to register A: 	$6.
Bit 1 (LSB) is equivant to the zero when being passed thru bit 0 for normal speaker output.
Bit 2 (MSB) is equivant to the value 32 or $21 when being passed thru bit 5 for normal speaker output.

example 1:
	and 	6			; masking music data for cassette port
	ld	($6800), a		; write it out.


example 2:
	ld	a, 4			; set bit 2 for tape
	ld 	($6800), a		; send to music port latch
 	LD	B,$12			; duration to hold
	DJNZ	$
	XOR	A			; wiggle reg A
	ld 	($6800), a		; and write it out again.

	ld	a, 2			; equivalent to sending ZERO to tape port even though this reads weird.
	ld 	($6800), a		; This will not work in any music player. Tested and verified.




d. Sound mods !
---------------
; 1. External Speaker taken from off internal Piezo.
; -----------------------------------------------
; Open up VZ/Laser. Drill a hoel suitable for a 3.5mm stereo socket near power switch.
; Install the stereo socket.
; May need to remove motherboard. Run two wires from the peizo to two inline 
; 0.1uf greencaps or tagulams capacitors, and then from the greencaps to the socket.
; Interestingly some 1-bit music methods do not trigger the peizo, but work fine with 
; amplified proper speakers. Wire centre of piezo to Left and Right channel, outside of
; piezo to common.                       
;                                         DISCLAIMER
;                      PLEASE NOTE : DO NOT WIRE THIS UP WITHOUT THE IN-SERIES CAPS !   
;                      YOU CAN EASILY BLOW THE GA CHIP ON A VZ300/LASER310. 
;                                     YOU HAVE BEEN WARNED.
;
;
;                        0.1uf
;          --------+------||------> 3.5mm socket "common"
; TO VZ            |
; board    ----+----------||------> 3.5mm socket. "LEFT and RIGHT"
;              |   |     0.1uf
;              |   |
;              |   |
;         ___________ <--piezo speaker.
;
;
;
;
;
;
; 2. Experimental Cassette port option.
------------------------------------
; Plug in a stereo 3.5mm socket connected to amplified speakers. Select this option
; and music will be output to the cassette port through to your doof-doof speakers.
;                       
;  VZ cassette   
;  port socket             _____________
;               /--,--,---|3.5mm stereo |
;               \--'--'---|_______plug__|
;
;                 ^  ^  ^
;                 |  |  |
;                 |  |  |____common
;                 |  |_______right channel       
;                 |__________left channel
;
;
;
;
;
;
; 3. Experimental Printer Port option.
------------------------------------
; Just connect up your VZ/Laser printer interface, then, connect up an amplified speaker
;  to D4 and ground. D4 being output data bit5, being pin 6 of the centronics plug.
;  Chose data line D4/bit5 since bit5 is the same data line on the $6800latch for the 
; internal speaker. May as well make it the same, since it just makes outputing data
; just that little bit easier.   LD ($6800), A    -->   OUT (14), A
;
; ------------   pin 1. strobe                                  pin 1 strobe
;             \  pin 2. D0
;              | pin 3. D1
;              | pin 4. D2
; 36 pin       | pin 5. D3                   0.1uf
; Centronics   | pin 6. D4              ---> -||- -->   connect to         
; Printer      | pin 7. D5                              amplified 
; Male plug    | .                                      speakers. 
; coming from  | .                                      Can add two 0.1uf caps in-line if
; VZ printer   | pin 16. SIGNAL GROUND  ---> -||- -->   you feel the need to.
;interface unit| .                           0.1uf      I haven't bothered and after all of my
;              | .                                      testing, I havent blown anything yet.
;             / 
; ------------  
;
;

0. Persons involved in early development
      a. Video Technology Staff
Not too much is known about the original development of the Laser series of computers.
One of the first David Ahl reviews shows an engineer working at a Laser 200 within Video Technology.
In the early to mid 2000's, a black and white photo was found of three Video Tech engineers holding a Laser 200. 
Unfortunately this photo was not ever kept.


      b. Wolfgang Maschke
A German fellow that started the German VZ200 fan club, and kept a large software repositry of German made VZ200 software.


      c. Greg Dubios & Trisha McNamara
Jason Oakley's interview with Greg:  https://bluebilby.com/dubois-mcnamara/

"I had many years of fun playing with the VZ machines. I built a network of 5 machines connected on a 100-metre cable. 
I wrote a networking program where each machine could access the drives on a Model III (which was the host). 
All machines could read/write/print to the Model III."


      d. Gerhard Wolf		
Gerhard wrote many of the Laser technical books (BASIC, DOS disassembly, ROM disassembly)


      f. Peng Xinmin
A Chinese gentleman that wrote the Chinese Laser technical bible manual.


      g. Larry Taylor
      h. Leslie Milburn
      i. Guy Thomason

1. VZ Editor Assembler (EDASM)
	1) Editor Assembler by Dubois and McNamara.  Hard coded fixed set for a VZ200 + 16k RAM pack system.
	2) Editor Assembler by Dubois and McNamara.  Tests for memory and adjusts accordingly.
	3) "Diskops" Patches - patch D & M Editor Assembler.
	4) Dave Mitchell Version. Patches D & M Editor Assembler.
	5) Russell Harrison Version - patches D & M Editor Assembler.
	6) Mark Harwood Version (EDASV1.2 & EDASV6.0X). patches D & M Editor Assembler.
	7) Brian Grieve Editor Assembler - Stand alone EDASM.
	8) Ian Webber's 16k Assembler and Editor.
	9) MASM-80. As used to assemble the vz serial ROM.
	10) MONITOR : (part of the china Utils), has an assemble & entry function.
	11) Monitor : Australian Personal Computer (APC) magainze. Basic type-in. Has an entry & assemble function.

(c) ZX BASIC
------------
URL: https://github.com/boriel/zxbasic      			http://www.boriel.com
     ZX BASIC assembler compiler was initially written for the use on th ZX Spectrum by Boriel. 
     With time, this has expanded to other Z80 platforms, including a small library for the VZ/Laser.
     In essence, the source can be a mixed of BASIC with assembly, and then assembled into a binary.



(d) MPAGD  (Multi Platform Arcade Game Development)
---------
URL: http://www.mpagd.com
     https://bluebilby.com/developing-for-vz-part-3/
     Starting with the ZX Spectrum, MPAGD is now a full complete games development suite for a number 
     of Z80 platforms, incluing the VZ/Laser, and even has support for the VZ's extended super hi-res 
     256x192 graphics modification modes.
     Jason & Kees performed a lot of work in this area creating a full and rich Laser/VZ Library for 
     MPAGD. Kees also has ported a large number of 256x192 games from the COCO 6847 range to the Laser/VZ
     which can be played in the VZEM emulator and on real hardwaer with the super-highres conversion.
     Most of Jason's released games between 2020-2024 have been written in MPAGD.
     example (Hero): https://jasonoakley.com/video-game/hero-for-vz200



(e) TRSE - RASCAL
-----------------
URL: http://www.lemonspawn.org
     https://bluebilby.com/developing-for-vz-part-4/
     Rascal is a pascal-like language, written for and compiled to a bunch of platforms with differing processors.
     Jason did a lot of work with Rascal, writing a rich Laser/VZ library for Rascal.
     Jason wrote Millipede in Rascal. See:   https://jasonoakley.com/programming/millipede


(f) UgBASIC
-----------
URL: https://ugbasic.iwashere.eu/
     An isomorphic language for retrocomputers.

     ugBASIC is an isomorphic and open source language for 8 bit retrocomputers. Fully documented 
     and designed to develop portable programs, without sacrificing efficiency. With a single source 
     it is therefore possible to create games for numerous 8 bit platforms. 
     ugBASIC IDE offers syntactic highlighting and an integration for error diagnostics: in case of 
     problems you are reported directly on the line with the error. It manages the program history 
     and it is multi-window. 

     It is still early days for the Laser VZ world, but UgBasic now supports (in Beta) our BASIC and 
     6847 graphics. Source code is written in a flavour of basic in UgBasics own IDE, and, will one
     day self-compile directly to a .VZ binary. In its current form, compilation is done manually.

Address Decode Tools - by Aaron Climas.
---------------------

Two quick helpers for experimenting with address decoding.

--------------
POKE 30862,201:POKE 30863,39:PRINT USR (0)	: Displays free memory.
PRINT PEEK(30897)+256*PEEK(30898)		: Displays Top of memory.

    $B800 -       - RAM, if VZ200 16kb modified RAM pack added.
    $B800 -       - RAM, if VZ200 64kb RAM pack added.

Most of the VZs BASIC words that were never enabled in the VZ have dedicated
processing routines in ROM. Each of these words is listed along with its token
code, its start address in the WORD TABLE, and the start address for the 
routine that processes it. The routine addresses are listed in decimal, then
in LO HI format (where address = LO + HI * 256) and in hex.

Words from END (token 128) to NEW (token 187) are all words that must begin
a BASIC statement if they are used at all. Some such as END,CLS,NEW,STOP must
stand alaone as the entire statement or be the primary word of an extended
statement.

Each of these words has a two byte pointer in the POINTER TABLE that follows 
the WORD TABLE. The pointer contains the address (in LO HI format) of the
routine that executes the word.

Words from SGN (token 215) to MID$ (token 250) are all words that cannot 
occur at the start of a basic statement. Each has a pointer in the POINTER
TABLE which proceeds the WORD TABLE, where again the pointer addresses a 
processing routine (execute!) for that word.

Words from TAB( (token 188) to < (token 214) do not have pointers in either of
the POINTER TABLES. They are executed by the primary words of statements in
which they occur. Strictly speaking it is the tokens that do all the dirty
work. 

When the program is RUN, tokens 205 to 208 and 212 to 214 designate words that 
display on screen as graphic characters (sort of like the ZX spectrum had).
The program is accurate. The first byte of each word being in inverse form
to mark the start of a new word. This is strictly true for words which begin
with alphabetical characters, the majority, but not for those few words that
consist of  nonalphabetical characters. A more accurate wording would be:

The first byte of each word being the ASCII character code + 128 to mark the
start of a new word. For alphabetical characters, adding 128 to the ASCII code
will yield the inverse character code. Most BASIC words begin with an 
alphabetical character, so most of the words in the WORD TABLE will begin with
an inverse character. However a few BASIC words consist of a single 
nonalphabetical character:   +,,*,/,^,>,=,<

They too are represented in the WORD TABLE by the ascii code + 128, but this
does not yield the inverse character codes. On the VZ, for those characters
with ASCII codes less than 64, the inverse character code is the ASCII code
+ 192. Still, when the VZ BASIC INTERPRETER comes across one of these words 
in the WORD TABLE, it subtracts 128 to get the ASCII code.

End of Communications RAM Region
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------

DOS ENTRY POINTS
a jump table to the dos subroutines is positioned at the fixed
address from 4008h to 4044h. the jump table contains the following elements:

4008	jp pwrin 	disk power on
400b	jp pwoff	disk power off
400e	jp error	error handling routine
4011	jp rdmap	read the track map of the disk
4014	jp clear	clear a sector of the disk
4017	jp svmap	save the track map to the disk
401a	jp init		initialises the disk
401d	jp csi		command string interpreter
4020	jp hex		convert ascii to hex
4023	jp idam		read the identification address mark
4026	jp create 	create an entry in directory
4029	jp map		search for empty sector
402c	jp search	search for file in directory
402f	jp find		search empty space in directory
4032	jp write	write a sector to disk
4035	jp read		read a sector from disk
4038	jp dly		delay ms in reg c
403b	jp stpin	step in
403e	jp stpout	step out
4041	jp dkload	load a file from disk
4044	jp saveob	save a file to disk




Supplied by Rhys Weatherley
----------------------------

Usually a peripheral also needs ROM support but the peripheral port does not make the memory 
bus accessible. Only peripherals that have support in the standard ROM's can be connected to 
the peripheral port. If the peripheral needs custom drivers, then it must be connected to the 
memory expansion / cartridge port, with the driver in external ROM.
System Variables in RAM

    $7800 - $7802 - Handler for RST $08, usually a JP or RET instruction.
    $7803 - $7805 - Handler for RST $10, usually a JP or RET instruction.
    $7806 - $7808 - Handler for RST $18, usually a JP or RET instruction.
    $7809 - $780B - Handler for RST $20, usually a JP or RET instruction.
    $780C - $780E - Handler for RST $28, usually a JP or RET instruction.
    $780F - $7811 - Handler for RST $30, usually a JP or RET instruction.
    $7820 - $7821 - Address of the cursor position between $7000 and $71FF.
    $783B - Copy of the output latch; the last value that was written to $6800 in I/O space.
    $787D - $787F - Interrupt handler.
    $788E, $788F - 16-bit address of the USR routine.
    $789C - Output device; 0 = screen, 1 = printer, -1 = cassette.
    $78A0, $78A1 - 16-bit address of the end of BASIC's stack and the start of the string variable storage area.
    $78A4, $78A5 - 16-bit address of the start of the BASIC program.
    $78B1, $78B2 - 16-bit address of the top of memory.
    $78F9, $78FA - 16-bit address of the end of the BASIC program and the start of the simple variables table.
    $78FB, $78FC - 16-bit address of the start of the dimensioned variables table.
    $7921, $7922 - 16-bit value that was passed to and from the USR routine.
    $79E8 - 8-bit index into the 64-byte BASIC input line buffer.
    $7AE9 - Default starting location for BASIC programs.



Useful subroutines in ROM
0000 - Reset Vector
Control jumps to here upon CPU reset.

0008 - RST $08
Handler for RST $08; jumps to the address $7800. By default this jumps to the "Examine String" routine at $1C96.

0049 - Keyboard Get
Waits for a character on the keyboard and returns it in A. Destroys F, BC, DE, and HL.

0050 - Screen Get Character
Gets the character under the cursor on the screen into A. Destroys HL.

01C9 - Clear Screen
Clears the screen and moves the cursor to the home position. Destroys AF, BC, DE, and HL.

033A - Character Output
Outputs the character in A to the screen. All registers are preserved.

058D - Print Character
Prints the character in C to the printer. Returns the printed character in A and C, and destroys F, B, DE, and HL.
Calling this routine with C = 0 will check the printer status in A only. Carry will be set if BREAK has been pressed.

05C4 - Check Printer Status
Returns the printer status byte in A. If bit 0 is set, the printer is busy. If bit 0 is not set, the printer is ready. BC, DE, and HL are preserved.

06A0 - Enable Interrupts and Enter BASIC
Enables interrupts and then jumps to the routine at $1A19.

1A19 - BASIC Entry Point
Entry point to the BASIC interpreter. ROM cartridges can jump to this location to re-enter BASIC when the cartridge program exits.

1C90 - Compare HL and DE
Compares HL and DE and sets the C and Z flags appropriately. HL is destroyed.
Normally this routine is accessed via RST $18.

1C96 - Examine String
Compares the byte at the address in HL with the value in the calling function. If the bytes are the same, HL is incremented and control returns. If the bytes differ, then jumps into BASIC with a "SYNTAX ERROR".
Normally this routine is accessed via RST $08. The following example checks if the string at HL is "ABC":

RST $08
DB  $41
RST $08
DB  $42
RST $08
DB  $43

1D78 - Check Next Character
Fetches the character at HL and clears the carry bit if it is alphabetic, or sets the carry bit if not. Whitespace is skipped automatically.
Normally this routine is accessed via RST $10. However, if DOS is running then issuing RST $10 will instead call into DOS.

28A7 - String Output
Writes the NUL-terminated string that is pointed to by HL to the screen. Destroys AF, BC, DE, and HL.

2EB8 - Interrupt Handler
Handles interrupts to the CPU on the "INT" line. Calls the user-supplied handler at $787D and then performs some internal housekeeping.
Because the "INT" line is hooked up to the frame sync signal of the MC6847 Video Display Generator (VDG), the interrupt will fire off at 50Hz on PAL systems when interrupts are enabled in the CPU.

2EF4 - Keyboard Scan
Scans the keyboard matrix and returns the key that is pressed in A. Or zero if no key is pressed. Destroys F, BC, DE, and HL.

2EFD - Keyboard Scan Once
Another keyboard scanning routine. No debouncing? No echo? TBD

308B - Character Output 2
Outputs the character in A to the screen. All registers are destroyed. The routine at $033A should be used by user programs instead of this internal routine.

3450 - Beep
Beep the speaker. Destroys AF, BC, and DE. HL is preserved.

345C - Sound Output
Plays a sound on the speaker. HL contains the pitch and BC contains the duration. Destroys AF, BC, DE, and HL.

3AE2 - Print CRLF
Sends a CRLF sequence to the printer. Destroys AF, BC, DE, and HL. If BREAK was pressed, then the carry flag will be set.



ROM pointers / offsets definition.
=====================================
0000 C  RST_00
0000 +  prevent interrupts
0002 +  continue at 0674H
0008 C  RST_08
0008 +	continue at 7800H and from there at 1C96H
0010 C  RST_10
0010 +	continue at 7803H and from there at 1D78H
0013 ;  DCB input (AF,DE)
0013 ;  I: DE = DCB address
0013 ;  O: A  = byte read
0013 C  INCH
0014 +  DCB type is input

##################################################################################################

============
(Q) Serials
============
The serial number listing. Started off as an idea to collect known serials, and has grown.
It appears as though Laser500, Laser700 also use the same sequential serial numbers.
From a random source it mentioned that batches of numbers were produced at different processing plants.
However after browsing the listing there appears to be no real meaningful or identifiable groups of numbers,
other than the cut off periods of the 300 and 310 generations. 


All units have 2k video which is included in the RAM totals below.
4k = 2k user RAM + 2k video RAM.
8k = 6k user RAM + 2k video RAM.
18k= 16k user RAM + 2k video RAM.
66k= 64k user RAM + 2k video RAM.

Note:-
"Laser200 generation 2":  Seltron style layout of PCB, ie, Laser310 circuit board in Laser200 housing.

"VZ300/Laser310 generation 1": Brown keys with no under labels on keys.  mid 1985-1986.  (Late 1984 for Germany!)
"VZ300/Laser310 generation 2": Brown keys with under labels on keys.     1986-1987.
"VZ300/Laser310 generation 3": beige/cream keyboard.                     1987 onwards.

Known to exist units with missing serial numbers unfortunately.
Some will be impossible to further trace into the future.

=====================
(X) Known lost items
=====================
- Photo of three CHinese gentleman (Engineers?) overlooking a Laser 100 or Laser200 computer. Perhaps taken in 1981 or 1982.
  One of the members found the image and posted it on to the VZEMU yahoogroups email list in the early to mid 2000's. 
  The author has searched and searched and has yet to find it.


- Many software titles have been lost to time, with even more source code lost to the ether thru time. Very sad when this
  happens, and this exact same thing still is happening today. People don't learn. They want the copyright and for others 
  to not see their source at the time. Then time slips by. And source code & binaries are then gone forever.
  Software source code and the resulting binary needs to be kept safe, secure for the future. This is why archiving is so
  important. 
  Known lost-to-time titles are listed within the software listing, found elsewhere.
  Imagine the joy in browsing PDF copies of the original printout source code of the DSE games with scribbles and notes 
  written all over.


- A few hardware kits have come and gone over the years, and are no longer in existance. The author is fairly confident that 
  there no longer exists any of the Hickman brothers VZ serial kits as an exmaple.  This was a whole replacement serial 
  interface, like that of the DSE kit, but on stereoids. It allowed for data and file transfer with the use of up to 300 baud modems. 


- DSE Games pack software are yet to be transferred over - uses copyright protection and this has yet to be bypassed.
  Track 1 or track 2 uses sectors numbered 16 to 31, instead of the standard 0 to 15.
  The four games packs need to be done sooner than later, since one day these floppy discs will just simply die due to age.






==========
(Y) Quirks
==========

* A giant wall sized VZ200 poster was created by Vtech, advertising the VZ200 computer.
"The unfair advantage for students of all ages". It can be found under the 'graphics' folder of the 'everything'
repositry. The image is quite sharp and clear, and makes for a nice A3 sized poster to pin on the wall.


* The April 1983 Australian Personal Computer magazine has the VZ200 pictured in full across the entire front cover.
  Apparently Tim Hartnell had quite a bit of pull in doing this.

* The New Zealand Dick Smith Electronics 1984 catalogue front cover has the VZ200 as the main photo.


* A VZ200 is shown on the cover of the Australian book "Celebrations - Living life to the full", released in 1986/87.


* In 2018 Pingo released a booklet telling of stories from the 1983-1985 era of the underground smuggling that 
occured with a family member - how the Laser was smuggled out of Germany and distributed in nearby neighbouring 
countries. Have a read, it makes for some very interesting reading. Can be found within the 'articles_books_mag' folder.


* Various ads for the VZ and Laser were in differing Electronics Magazines across a number of countries advertising
  these machines as learning computers; some advertised as the VZ as being free when an intro course was bought and ran.
  These courses were offered in Australia, New Zealand and even in Italy and Spain.


* The VZ300 gen3 was shown in one episode of 'A Country Practise', an Australian TV drama series through the 1980's 
and early 1990's. The late model VZ is shown with a fake name 'HEK' placed on top of the VZ300 logo. 
Actor Bob Hatfield can be seen attempting to read the DOS manual, whilst Shirley can be seen using the VZ performing budgets.
Husband Police Officer (name escapes me?) walks in and watches the computer display a love letter to Shirley, signed 
Barry the computer.    - A Country Practise. Series 7, Episodes 69 and 72.


* In 1990 or 1991, a 17 year old VZ enthusiast, Ben Hobson, connected up his soldered-together voice synthesizer  
to his VZ300, worked at his sylabil pronounciation program until he was happy with it, then rang the Muswellbrook 
(NSW) radio station 2NM on one middle-of-the-week song request night, and asked via the speech synth:
"Can I please request Do the Bartman" via the synthesiser to the radio DJ. 
The DJ laughed and had to ask what was being said. A radio-to-cassette recording of this still exists that was 
taken by the FAQ author.


* In 1991 a Holden Gemini was featured in Street Machine Magzine that was running a VZ200 for the infotainment system.
A VZ200 keyboard is screwed on to the inside of the glove box lid, and a bad quality photo shows what looks to be a
6847-ish display on a small six-inch CRT television.
If this URL ever disappears, it will be available through the Wayback machine via archive.org
https://www.streetmachine.com.au/features/high-tech-holden-td-gemini-wagon-ortron


* A Youtube exists of the FAQ author running a VZ300 in his car, dangerously playing Circus on the stereo LCD display 
  whilst driving. Another youtube exists showing playing 1-bit music from a VZ300 through the same stereo cranked up.


* A Youtube exists of an overly enthusiastic VZ luser dancing to 2018AD on a large theatre auditorium screen within the
  Quirindi (NSW) Royal Theatre.

LYSCo - the Leon Young Software Company, from Bunbury, Western Australia. Seems to have existed from 1984-1986, supporting a range of computers including the VZ200/300, Amstrad and Commodore (C-16, Plus 4) machines. Also sold hardware such as hard disks, printers, monitors and IBM PC clones.


https://github.com/acutesoftware/old_C_code/tree/master/BAS_Lost_Sword

DSVZ200
https://github.com/PhoenixInteractiveNL/emuDownloadCenter/wiki/Emulator-dsvz200


Lost Sword
https://github.com/acutesoftware/old_C_code/tree/master/BAS_Lost_Sword


I know this is an old thread but I can provide some insight into this game Dracula’s Castle. It was developed by a high school kid, David Boggs, in Tamworth Australia and sold to Dick Smith electronics to publish and distribute. It was my brother. He happily declared the plant monster was based on me as a brother would do...





https://github.com/EtchedPixels/EmulatorKit   RC2014 environment

 

VZ300
Emulates the VZ200 or VZ300 systems. Can also emulate the SD card add on and the Australian banked video memory extension.



https://github.com/yuanb/vzem
This repository is to updating the VZ300 emulator 'vzem', originally authored by Guy Thomason, to the most recent versions of Visual Studio, the Platform Toolset, and the Windows SDK. Additionally, it aims to rectify any compiler warnings.

 

https://github.com/lindoran/RC39-Backplane-System-for-VZ200
This project is an RC39-based backplane designed for building home computer workalikes.
The current focus is on creating a VZ200 clone workalike.

 

https://github.com/lindoran/VZ200-Level-II-BASIC-Disasembly
A project to document the VZ200 ROM
Maybe this gets good enough we can get it to assemble, for now I would just like to be able to understand how the rom works a bit better. We can lean heavy on the existing german disasembly found at bushys. I'll also back it up in the /doc folder. You can also find a real crappy formatter for z80dasm output in /src. Its mostly ai generated code but works. I haven't bothered to teach myself Gehdra, I suppose that is better suited for projects like this. I like making my own toolchains and its simple enough to work just in vscode which makes my brain happy.

 

https://github.com/lambdamikel/vz200-lazarus-daughter-board
Laser (VTech, Dick Smith, Sanyo Video Technologies, ...) VZ-200 Lazarus Daughter Board
Background

Sometimes, VZ-200 repairs go really bad - if you have to remove the 6116 SRAM or ROM chips during a repair due to faulty RAM / ROM, chances are that you are going to cause damage to the motherboard, as the PCB quality is very low. So far, everybody attempting to remove the ROM chips from the board has a least lifted a couple of pads and/or damage some PCB traces, resulting in bodge-wires.

 

https://github.com/MiSTer-devel/Laser310_MiSTer/
Laser310 port to MiSTer
//  Copyright (c) 2019,2020 Alan Steremberg – alanswx

Atzeco Adventure
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C10159/Atzeco+Adventure.html
Synopsis:The year is 1520 A.D. You find yourself in the Anahuac Valley. Streams flow down the sides of the distant 
mountains to form five lakes extending 80 km in length. Your goal is to explore the surrounding villages, find your 
way into the city of Tenochtitlan and go to Montezuma's room. A knowledge of Aztec history and culture will prove useful.
Notes:This game was originally published by SOFIN s.r.l. as two BASIC type-in listings for the Laser 310 and Laser 500 
in Laser Computer Club, vol. 3, no. 4, August 1988, pp. 10-25.
The listing for the Laser 310 was impossible to enter and play without making modifications. See the solution for details.
The game was translated to English in 2023.


Aztec Adventure
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C10160/Aztec+Adventure.html
Synopsis:The year is 1520 A.D. You find yourself in the Anahuac Valley. Streams flow down the sides of the distant 
mountains to form five lakes extending 80 km in length. Your goal is to explore the surrounding villages, find your 
way into the city of Tenochtitlan and go to Montezuma's room. A knowledge of Aztec history and culture will prove useful.
Notes:This game was originally published as an Italian game (under the title Atzeco Adventure) by SOFIN s.r.l. 
The magazine featured two BASIC type-in listings for the Laser 310 and Laser 500 in Laser Computer Club, 
vol. 3, no. 4, August 1988, pp. 10-25.
This updated Laser 310 version was bug fixed and translated to English by Garry Francis in 2023.



Atlantic Adventure
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C4628/Atlantic+Adventure.html
Synopsis:You're stranded on an island. To escape you first have to find the treasure which is located somewhere on the island.
Notes:This was originally published by Roeske Verlag as a BASIC type-in listing for the TRS-80 in Homecomputer, vol. 2, no. 3, March 1984, pp. 30–34.
It was translated to Dutch by Gordon Rahman.
It was ported to the VZ200 by Wolfgang Wesemann.



Arthur's Day Out
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C9133/Arthur%27s+Day+Out.html
Synopsis:You find yourself in a broom closet with a hangover. Your objective is to find your way out of the broom closet
and complete a series of somewhat disjointed and unrelated tasks in order to achieve the full score of 200 points. 
Only when the game ends will you find out what this was all about. Maybe they weren't disjointed and unrelated after all.
Notes:This was an entry in PunyJam #1, where it placed 7th out of 9 entries.
The game appears to be a homage to 'The Hitchhiker's Guide to the Galaxy', based on the title and the objects found in the first room.
The VZ version is available from Jason "Blue Bilby" Oakley's website.

things going on inside the mansion until it was too late. Now you've to find a way to escape alive.
Notes

Chasm Capers
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C4623/Chasm+Capers.html
Synopsis:You are trapped inside a cave and have to search a way out. Collect all the treasures before you escape 
but watch out for the bats and the pirate.

Notes:Originally published as a type-in on pages 32-42 of the book Second Book of Programs published by Dick Smith Management 
for their VZ200 computer. The game requires the 16K Rampack.


Chasm Capers
Walkthrough by Alex Dijkstra for the Dick Smith VZ200

N, GET LANTERN, W,  S, GET SAND, N, GET BOTTLE, E, DROP SAND (the ground no longer is slippery and
you can go south), DROP LANTERN, FILL BOTTLE (with water), GET SEED, S, DROP SEED, POUR WATER (onto
seed. a huge plant grows), DROP BOTTLE, N, GET GOLD, S, DROP GOLD, GET ROCK, N, GET LANTERN, GET
SAND (to block this exit for the pirate),  W, DROP SAND, GET WAND, S, S,  PLUGH VENT (with rock),
DROP LANTERN, GET SILVER, GET DIAMONDS, WAVE WAND (a bridge appears), E, WAVE WAND (bridge vanishes
and blocks pirate), DROP WAND, GET GOLD, CLIMB PLANT.
Well done. You've escaped the caves with all the treasures.

It was apparently published in issue 23 of the VZDU newsletter.



Danger Mouse
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C4630/Danger+Mouse.html
This game has many random elements including the object locations. Furthermore it has several misspellings like latter (ladder) and diamont (diamond).
Listed on various sites as both DangerMouse and Danger Mouse. It's unclear which title the author intended.
The game is also listed online as Diamond. Jim Gerrie has produced a TRS-80 MC-10 version of this game.


Down Under
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C5719/Down+Under.html


Drakulas Diamenten
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C2730/Drakulas+Diamanten.html
Synopsis:All hell is breaking loose at Interpol: The valuable diamonds belonging to billionaire NJ Sirmel's wife disappeared three days ago. Mrs. Sirmel was found dead on Monday morning, robbed of both her blood and her jewellery, lying on the knee-high Persian rug, the window open, and the perpetrator gone.
Notes:This game was originally published as a type-in for ZX-81 in the October 1983 issue of Homecomputer magazine, pp.30-35.
The Spectrum and C64 versions may be unofficial ports. There are two different versions of the C64 game; one is usually listed under the title Dracula the other as Draculas Diamanten. The main difference seems to be in tweaks to the graphics.
There is a (possibly unofficial) Laser/VZ200 version of the game; dated 1985 and attributed to Wolfgang Wesemann; which is mentioned in the Laser Fan Club newsletter, issue 10.
Frank Gerbig has produced an unofficial port of this game to Inform; see here.

Haunted Mansion	
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C10039/Haunted+Mansion.html
It's possible that this is an adaptation of one of the many "haunted house" games in other BASICs. 
At this point the author is presumed to be Scott Le Brun. It may possibly be related to the design here. 
It looks likely that the game was based on, or at least heavily inspired, by the Usborne type-in, Haunted House.
Sold at least as early as 1985 or 1986 through Scott Le Brun's VZDU and also other VZ user group publishers 
such as VsoftwareZ; as it's mentioned in LE VZ200/300 OOP issue 10 from February 1986.

The High Mountains
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C2908/High+Mountains%2C+The.html
Synopsis:This game can be played 2 different ways :
If you are either a MASTER or a TRIPOD then the object of the game is to destroy all the other HUMANS.
If you are not a MASTER or a TRIPOD then you must collect the appropriate objects and carry out the tasks to 
enable you to destroy the Golden City.
When you think you have the required objects in their correct form, move to the Golden City and fire away.
Notes:Support for up to five players.
Inspired by John Christopher's The Tripods book series.
Originally published as a type-in game in issue 22 of Personal Computer News.
An unofficial port exists for the Microbee.
In 2014, the game was ported to the TRS-80 MC-10, TRS-80 Coco, and the Dragon 32 by Jim Gerrie.

The Lost Sword
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C10077/Lost+Sword%2C+The.html
Synopsis:The land of Erindor is slowly being destroyed by evil. You must enter the ancient caves to recover the lost sword so that the evil can be defeated.
Notes:benkid77 has produced a YouTube walkthrough of this game.

Solution for the VZ200 text adventure "The Lost Sword" by Duncan Murray, 
published by LYSCo (Leon Young Software Company). 

Note: Only the first three letters of each word are needed but they are shown 
in full here for clarity. 

Comments are in round brackets. Command sequences in square brackets are 
optional but do reveal more about the game.

GET ROCK, S, [LOOK AT GOBLIN], THROW ROCK (kills the goblin), GET KEY, N, 
UNLOCK GATE, DROP KEY, N, GET PLANK, S, E, N, DROP PLANK, S, S, S, W, GET NET, 
E, N, E, GET KEY, GET MONGOOSE, W, N, N, E, DROP MONGOOSE (*note1), DROP NET, 
THROW MONGOOSE (it’s the only way to drop it), GET EARMUFFS, W, S, S, E, S, 
GET MIRROR, E, N, N, UNLOCK DOOR, THROW KEY (*note2), [L, E (*note3), LOOK AT 
PICTURES (*note4), W], S, E, S (*note5), HOLD MIRROR (medussa turns to stone), 
DROP MIRROR, GET SPEAR, N, W, N, N, E, THROW SPEAR, GET KEY, W, W, W, S, [LOOK 
AT SPHINX (you get a riddle)], SAY MAN (the sphinx disappears), UNLOCK DOOR, L, 
E, SAY MELLON (... YOU HAVE RECOVERED THE ANCIENT SWORD OF THE KINGS … 
C O N G R A T U L A T I O N S; *note6)

*note1:- The mongoose kills the snake, but it still remains in your inventory 
even after dropping it. It can be released with THROW MONGOOSE.

*note2:- "THROW KEY" avoids a bug with "DROP KEY" where the wrong key is 
dropped into the current location even if you don’t have it.

*note3:- Room of a 1000 words. You don’t actually need to enter this room to 
complete the game in a minimal solution, but you do need to see it at least 
once for vital clues.

*note4:- There is a picture of a baby crawling, a man walking and an old man 
with a stick. This illustrates the answer ("MAN" or "HUMAN") that you will need

later to solve the riddle of the sphinx. Also, the word "MELLON" is revealed, 
needed at the very end of the game.

*note5:- The "strange woman" here is actually the "medussa" (sic). This is 
revealed if you "LOOK AT WOMAN", killing the player in the process (unless you 
are prepared). Although the only way you could have been prepared (with "HOLD 
MIRROR") is if you already knew the woman was medusa, by dying in a previous 
game.

*note6:- Also the messages "YOU ARE NOW READY TO FIND & KILL FEAMOR" and 
"PLEASE START THE TAPE" appear on winning. They indicate another game was on 
the original tape, meant to be played after this one. This second game is 
missing in action as far as I know. At the time of writing, I only have a 
snapshot file of The Lost Sword, rather than a tape file. 

benkid77 - 18 January 2024

(C) CASA 1999 - 2026

EXAMINE BED (you find a newspaper), GET NEWSPAPER, READ NEWSPAPER (you learn you are searching for
the kidnapped author Robin M.), DROP NEWSPAPER, EXAMINE FIREPLACE (you find ashes), GET ASHES,
EXAMINE ASHES (you find a nail), GET NAIL, OPEN DOOR (with nail), DROP ASHES, DROP NAIL, GO DOOR, GO
SHED, GET TOOLBOX, GET LANTERN, GET ROPE, GET SHOVEL, GO DOOR, CLIMB LADDER, DROP TOOLBOX (the
toolbox breaks open), CLIMB LADDER, GET HAMMER, GO HUT, EXAMINE FLOOR (it is rotten), OPEN FLOOR
(with hammer), EXAMINE FLOOR (you find a map), GET MAP, GO DOOR, FOLLOW MAP, GO BOAT, LOOK, LOOK
(you hear a powerful roar), ROW (with shovel),  DROP MAP, DROP SHOVEL, THROW ROPE (the rope has
caught fast on the cliff), EXAMINE BUSH (you find some berries), GET BERRIES, EXAMINE BERRIES (a
strange type wit immunizing effects), EAT BERRIES, MOVE STONE (the berries protect you against the
snake bite and you find a compass), GET COMPASS, CLIMB ROPE, EXAMINE SIGN, GO SOUTH (you have to
carry the compass to walk around), GO SOUTH, EXAMINE BOX (you find a bottle, a lighter and a bomb),
GET BOMB, GET LIGHTER, GO NORTH, GO NORTH, GO EAST, GO EAST, OPEN MINE (with hammer), DROP HAMMER,
GO EAST, GET DYNAMITE, THROW DYNAMITE (the entrance caves in), LIGHT LANTERN (with lighter), GO
SOUTH (without a lit lantern you die), GO SOUTH (I hear a strange hum), LIGHT DYNAMITE (you light
the bomb with the lighter and a wall collapses), GO EAST, GO EAST , GO NORTH, GO EAST, GO EAST,
EXTINGUISH LAMP, GO OPENING, CLIMB CLIFF, GO SOUTH, GO SOUTH, GET BOTTLE, THROW BOTTLE (it
shatters),GET FRAGMENTS,  GO NORTH, GO NORTH, GO EAST, GO EAST, LIGHT LANTERN, GO NORTH, SWITCH
DOWN, GO EAST, GO NORTH, GO NORTH, EXAMINE ROBIN, CUT TIES (with fragments), GO SOUTH, GO SOUTH, GO
WEST, SWITCH ON, SWITCH ON, GO EAST, GO EAST. 
You have solved the adventure.(C) CASA 1999 - 2026

Pharoah's Tomb
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C10076/Pharoah%27s+Tomb.html

Synopsis:Your uncle was a great archaeologist. In his will, he left you a map showing how to get 
to the tomb of the last pharaoh of Egypt. A letter explained that three jewels (an emerald, a 
sapphire and a diamond) are the keys to gain entry to the tomb.
After much hardship, your expedition has reached the tomb, but the entrance caves in behind you. 
Your uncle warned you that no one has ever entered the tomb and lived to return. Can you be the 
first? You are left alone in the dark with only a torch to guide you.

The Secret of Volcano Island		
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C10062/Secret+of+Volcano+Island%2C+The.html
This was published by VeeZed Down Under Club as a BASIC type-in listing in VZDU, issue 40, January/March 1993, pp. 4-7.
The Secret of Volcano Island
By Owen Ross
Published by by VeeZed Down Under Club in 'VZDU', issue 40, January/March 1993, pp. 4-7
For VZ200/300

Solution by Garry Francis
----------------------------------------
This is a minimal solution to a fairly simple game. Your objective is to find three treasures and
return them to your starting location before the volcano erupts. The eruption occurs after 75 moves.
There are some guess-the-verb issues that will chew into your time. If you prefer not to play
guess-the-verb, you can find all the verbs listed at lines 3120-3140 of the BASIC source code.

The treasures aren't clearly identified. If you find an object that doesn't appear to
serve any purpose, then it's most likely a treasure. You won't know until you finish the
game. Speaking of which, you have to type Q (for QUIT) to finish the game once you've returned
with all the treasures. Some optional (but significant) commands are shown in parentheses. These can
be omitted once you know what to do.

E,E,E,GET MAGIC RING (first treasure),E,EXAMINE RIVER (you find a key),GET KEY,W,N,UNLOCK DOOR (with key, adds exit to north)
N,W,GET COIN (second treasure),E,N,E,GET GUN,W,S,S,S,W,W,W,N,FIRE GUN (light house is no longer guarded, adds exit to north)
(Note: There is a bug here. You can actually go north without shooting the invisible guard, but that
would presumably be cheating. To fix it, change GN=5 to PN=5 in line 228.)
N,,U,GET MATCHES,D,E,S,E,GET ROPE,W,N,N,W,N,GET AXE,S,E,N,E,LAY ROPE (good work if you guessed that one, adds exit to east)
E,(EXAMINE TREE) (it looks good for making boats),CUT TREE (with axe, you can see some wood),GET WOOD
E,BUILD BOAT (with the wood, a boat appears),LAUNCH BOAT (boat disappears, but you don't move anywhere),E
S (rocks block the way back),S,GET DYNAMITE,S,W,GET MAP (third treasure),E,N,N,LIGHT DYNAMITE (with matches, adds exit to north)
N,W,W,W,W,S,S,W,S,S,Q,***THE END***
(C) CASA 1999 - 2026

VZDU Adventure
https://solutionarchive.com/game/id%2C10044/VZDU+Adventure.html
This is an untitled, partial adventure that was featured in a series of articles written by 
David Wood on adventure writing for the VZ Down Under newsletter in 1990-91.
A design for the adventure is included in issue 27. Further segments of the adventure code, 
and suggestions for expansion, appear across the articles in issue 28, 29, 30, 31 and 32.
The complete adventure may have been included on one of the VZDU public domain disks.

Die Zitadelle des Zauberers

O, N, SETZEN (Sometimes the dwarf and the orc will tell you the codeword to enter the cidadel. This
is randomly determined at the beginning of the game. If they don't do this you'll have to
go : S, W, W, STAERKEZAUBER, O, N, O) , N, REDE (the whirlwind leaves you alone), W,  W, NEHME
BEEREN, O, enter  codeword and the Rhino-man will let you pass, N, KLINGEL, O, O, S, SETZE, REDE
(you get an amulett), N, N, MIDASZAUBER, W, W, N, O, O, LESE BUECHER, 2 (note down the combination
of the door), W, W, W, N, GEBE AMULETT, GEBE BEEREN, O, enter combination you read in the library.

Du hast es geschaft der Zauberer ausser gefecht zu setzen!
